I think… it’s mostly the way it looks, if I’m being honest. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.

That probably sounds a bit silly, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the scar, even when you're not in situations where it might be noticed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I try not to make too much of it. It just sort of pops into my mind, even when I’m not doing anything where anyone would see it. I’ll think, “It’s probably not really that bad,” but then I picture being at church or playing bridge and feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me. I know that may not be entirely reasonable, but it’s hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they noticed the scar?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’d judge me exactly… maybe that sounds too strong. It’s more that I keep feeling like if they notice it, their eyes will go right to my wrist and that’ll be the first thing they see about me now. \n\nI do know, at least in my head, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. But at church or bridge, I still worry people are noticing it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of the scar that bothers you, or is it more about what you think it might mean to others about you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly the way it looks, if I’m being honest. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I suppose that’s more about the appearance than anything it “means,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist with the scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly just the wrist, yes. I don’t really have other parts of my body I worry about in the same way. I mean, I know the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it… like it’s the first thing they notice now. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I almost feel silly bringing it up, but... I’ve been having a hard time not thinking about this scar on my wrist. I know it’s probably not as noticeable as it seems to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I feel like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you help with, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your scar been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still managing, mostly. But I have been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work because I get sort of caught up trying to make sure my wrist is covered or not noticeable before I go in or send things off.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped a few choir practices and some theater volunteer things, which I don’t like admitting. It’s just… I start worrying people will notice it, and then it feels easier not to go. That probably sounds a bit silly, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the scar, even when you're not in situations where it might be noticed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I try not to make too much of it. It just sort of pops into my mind, even when I’m not doing anything where anyone would see it. I’ll think, “It’s probably not really that bad,” but then I picture being at church or playing bridge and feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me. I know that may not be entirely reasonable, but it’s hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they noticed the scar?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’d judge me exactly… maybe that sounds too strong. It’s more that I keep feeling like if they notice it, their eyes will go right to my wrist and that’ll be the first thing they see about me now. \n\nI do know, at least in my head, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. But at church or bridge, I still worry people are noticing it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of the scar that bothers you, or is it more about what you think it might mean to others about you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly the way it looks, if I’m being honest. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I suppose that’s more about the appearance than anything it “means,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist with the scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly just the wrist, yes. I don’t really have other parts of my body I worry about in the same way. I mean, I know the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it… like it’s the first thing they notice now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the appearance of the scar that bothers you, such as its size, shape, or color?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s one specific thing like the shape or color… maybe just that it’s there and visible on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not as noticeable as it feels to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just get this feeling that people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe that sounds a bit silly, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar's appearance is greater than how others actually perceive it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it probably is. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is likely not as bad as it looks to me, and I’m trying not to make too much of it.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s… not unbearable, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. But it does bother me more than I’d like to admit, I suppose.

I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice now. So yes, it causes me some distress… mostly embarrassment and feeling self-conscious. Does that answer what you mean?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I almost feel silly bringing it up, but... I’ve been having a hard time not thinking about this scar on my wrist. I know it’s probably not as noticeable as it seems to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I feel like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you help with, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your scar been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still managing, mostly. But I have been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work because I get sort of caught up trying to make sure my wrist is covered or not noticeable before I go in or send things off.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped a few choir practices and some theater volunteer things, which I don’t like admitting. It’s just… I start worrying people will notice it, and then it feels easier not to go. That probably sounds a bit silly, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the scar, even when you're not in situations where it might be noticed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I try not to make too much of it. It just sort of pops into my mind, even when I’m not doing anything where anyone would see it. I’ll think, “It’s probably not really that bad,” but then I picture being at church or playing bridge and feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me. I know that may not be entirely reasonable, but it’s hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they noticed the scar?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’d judge me exactly… maybe that sounds too strong. It’s more that I keep feeling like if they notice it, their eyes will go right to my wrist and that’ll be the first thing they see about me now. \n\nI do know, at least in my head, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. But at church or bridge, I still worry people are noticing it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of the scar that bothers you, or is it more about what you think it might mean to others about you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly the way it looks, if I’m being honest. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I suppose that’s more about the appearance than anything it “means,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist with the scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly just the wrist, yes. I don’t really have other parts of my body I worry about in the same way. I mean, I know the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it… like it’s the first thing they notice now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the appearance of the scar that bothers you, such as its size, shape, or color?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s one specific thing like the shape or color… maybe just that it’s there and visible on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not as noticeable as it feels to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just get this feeling that people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe that sounds a bit silly, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar's appearance is greater than how others actually perceive it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it probably is. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is likely not as bad as it looks to me, and I’m trying not to make too much of it.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe they don’t really see it that way, but it feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about your scar is causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. But it does bother me more than I’d like to admit, I suppose.\n\nI know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice now. Maybe they don’t really see it that way, but it feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about your scar is causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. But it does bother me more than I’d like to admit, I suppose.\n\nI know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice now. So yes, it causes me some distress… mostly embarrassment and feeling self-conscious. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there something about your appearance, aside from the scar, that you feel is flawed or defective?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is likely not as bad as it looks to me, and I’m trying not to make too much of it.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe they don’t really see it that way, but it feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about your scar is causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. But it does bother me more than I’d like to admit, I suppose.\n\nI know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice now. So yes, it causes me some distress… mostly embarrassment and feeling self-conscious. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there something about your appearance, aside from the scar, that you feel is flawed or defective?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I’m 64, so of course there are little things I don’t love, but nothing I’d call flawed or defective.\n\nIt’s really just the scar that’s been on my mind. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the scar makes you imperfect in some way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I feel silly saying it that way. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. So I suppose it does make me feel… less put together, maybe imperfect. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you help with, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your scar been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still managing, mostly. But I have been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work because I get sort of caught up trying to make sure my wrist is covered or not noticeable before I go in or send things off.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped a few choir practices and some theater volunteer things, which I don’t like admitting. It’s just… I start worrying people will notice it, and then it feels easier not to go. That probably sounds a bit silly, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the scar, even when you're not in situations where it might be noticed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I try not to make too much of it. It just sort of pops into my mind, even when I’m not doing anything where anyone would see it. I’ll think, “It’s probably not really that bad,” but then I picture being at church or playing bridge and feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me. I know that may not be entirely reasonable, but it’s hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they noticed the scar?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’d judge me exactly… maybe that sounds too strong. It’s more that I keep feeling like if they notice it, their eyes will go right to my wrist and that’ll be the first thing they see about me now. \n\nI do know, at least in my head, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. But at church or bridge, I still worry people are noticing it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of the scar that bothers you, or is it more about what you think it might mean to others about you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly the way it looks, if I’m being honest. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I suppose that’s more about the appearance than anything it “means,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist with the scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly just the wrist, yes. A bit, yes… I hate to admit that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m making excuses.

With my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in a couple of times because I get distracted fussing over my wrist or thinking about whether someone might notice it. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I almost feel silly bringing it up, but... I’ve been having a hard time not thinking about this scar on my wrist. I know it’s probably not as noticeable as it seems to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I feel like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you help with, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your scar been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still managing, mostly. But I have been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work because I get sort of caught up trying to make sure my wrist is covered or not noticeable before I go in or send things off.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped a few choir practices and some theater volunteer things, which I don’t like admitting. It’s just… I start worrying people will notice it, and then it feels easier not to go. That probably sounds a bit silly, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the scar, even when you're not in situations where it might be noticed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I try not to make too much of it. It just sort of pops into my mind, even when I’m not doing anything where anyone would see it. I’ll think, “It’s probably not really that bad,” but then I picture being at church or playing bridge and feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me. I know that may not be entirely reasonable, but it’s hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they noticed the scar?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’d judge me exactly… maybe that sounds too strong. It’s more that I keep feeling like if they notice it, their eyes will go right to my wrist and that’ll be the first thing they see about me now. \n\nI do know, at least in my head, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. But at church or bridge, I still worry people are noticing it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of the scar that bothers you, or is it more about what you think it might mean to others about you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly the way it looks, if I’m being honest. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I suppose that’s more about the appearance than anything it “means,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist with the scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly just the wrist, yes. I don’t really have other parts of my body I worry about in the same way. I mean, I know the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it… like it’s the first thing they notice now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the appearance of the scar that bothers you, such as its size, shape, or color?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s one specific thing like the shape or color… maybe just that it’s there and visible on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not as noticeable as it feels to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just get this feeling that people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe that sounds a bit silly, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar's appearance is greater than how others actually perceive it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it probably is. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is likely not as bad as it looks to me, and I’m trying not to make too much of it.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe they don’t really see it that way, but it feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about your scar is causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. But it does bother me more than I’d like to admit, I suppose.\n\nI know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice now. So yes, it causes me some distress… mostly embarrassment and feeling self-conscious. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there something about your appearance, aside from the scar, that you feel is flawed or defective?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I’m 64, so of course there are little things I don’t love, but nothing I’d call flawed or defective.\n\nIt’s really just the scar that’s been on my mind. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the scar makes you imperfect in some way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I feel silly saying it that way. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. So I suppose it does make me feel… less put together, maybe imperfect. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about the scar when it pops into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it can be difficult, though I wouldn’t say it takes over completely.\n\nIt’s more that once I notice it, or even think about being at church or bridge, my mind sort of goes back to it. I’ll tell myself, “It’s probably not as noticeable as I think,” but then I still feel like everyone’s eyes are going right to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they see. So yes, it can be hard to shake off sometimes. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the scar been interfering with your ability to concentrate on other things you need to do?\n\nPatient: A bit, yes… I hate to admit that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m making excuses.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in a couple of times because I get distracted fussing over my wrist or thinking about whether someone might notice it. It’s just… I start worrying people will notice it, and then it feels easier not to go. It just sort of pops into my mind, even when I’m not doing anything where anyone would see it. I’ll think, “It’s probably not really that bad,” but then I picture being at church or playing bridge and feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me. I know that may not be entirely reasonable, but it’s hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they noticed the scar?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’d judge me exactly… maybe that sounds too strong. It’s more that I keep feeling like if they notice it, their eyes will go right to my wrist and that’ll be the first thing they see about me now. \n\nI do know, at least in my head, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. But at church or bridge, I still worry people are noticing it. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I suppose that’s more about the appearance than anything it “means,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist with the scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly just the wrist, yes. I don’t really have other parts of my body I worry about in the same way. I mean, I know the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it… like it’s the first thing they notice now. I know, logically, it’s probably not as noticeable as it feels to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just get this feeling that people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is likely not as bad as it looks to me, and I’m trying not to make too much of it.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe they don’t really see it that way, but it feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about your scar is causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. But it does bother me more than I’d like to admit, I suppose.\n\nI know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice now. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I don’t want to overstate it, because I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me.\n\nBut it does sort of pop in when I’m trying to pay attention to something else, especially if I’m thinking about church or bridge. I’ll start wondering if people are looking at it, like their eyes go right to my wrist and that’s the first thing they notice about me now. I try to tell myself that’s probably not true, but the thought still comes in anyway. Is that what you mean by intruding?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're thinking about the scar more often than you want to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or bridge I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… more than I’d like, I suppose.

I know, realistically, the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. But when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.

I’m not sure if that sounds silly, but… yes, I do think about it more than I want to.

I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you help with, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your scar been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still managing, mostly. But I have been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work because I get sort of caught up trying to make sure my wrist is covered or not noticeable before I go in or send things off.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped a few choir practices and some theater volunteer things, which I don’t like admitting. It’s just… I start worrying people will notice it, and then it feels easier not to go. It just sort of pops into my mind, even when I’m not doing anything where anyone would see it. It’s more that I keep feeling like if they notice it, their eyes will go right to my wrist and that’ll be the first thing they see about me now. \n\nI do know, at least in my head, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. But at church or bridge, I still worry people are noticing it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of the scar that bothers you, or is it more about what you think it might mean to others about you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly the way it looks, if I’m being honest. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I suppose that’s more about the appearance than anything it “means,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist with the scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly just the wrist, yes. I don’t really have other parts of my body I worry about in the same way. I know, logically, it’s probably not as noticeable as it feels to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just get this feeling that people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the scar makes you imperfect in some way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I feel silly saying it that way. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… maybe I would feel less self-conscious, yes. I don’t want to say it would fix everything about how I feel, because that sounds a little too neat.

I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you help with, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your scar been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still managing, mostly. But I have been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work because I get sort of caught up trying to make sure my wrist is covered or not noticeable before I go in or send things off.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped a few choir practices and some theater volunteer things, which I don’t like admitting. It’s just… I start worrying people will notice it, and then it feels easier not to go. That probably sounds a bit silly, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the scar, even when you're not in situations where it might be noticed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I try not to make too much of it. It just sort of pops into my mind, even when I’m not doing anything where anyone would see it. I’ll think, “It’s probably not really that bad,” but then I picture being at church or playing bridge and feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me. I know that may not be entirely reasonable, but it’s hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they noticed the scar?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’d judge me exactly… maybe that sounds too strong. It’s more that I keep feeling like if they notice it, their eyes will go right to my wrist and that’ll be the first thing they see about me now. \n\nI do know, at least in my head, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. But at church or bridge, I still worry people are noticing it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of the scar that bothers you, or is it more about what you think it might mean to others about you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly the way it looks, if I’m being honest. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I suppose that’s more about the appearance than anything it “means,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist with the scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly just the wrist, yes. I don’t really have other parts of my body I worry about in the same way. I mean, I know the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it… like it’s the first thing they notice now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the appearance of the scar that bothers you, such as its size, shape, or color?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s one specific thing like the shape or color… maybe just that it’s there and visible on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not as noticeable as it feels to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just get this feeling that people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe that sounds a bit silly, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar's appearance is greater than how others actually perceive it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it probably is. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is likely not as bad as it looks to me, and I’m trying not to make too much of it.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe they don’t really see it that way, but it feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about your scar is causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. But it does bother me more than I’d like to admit, I suppose.\n\nI know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice now. So yes, it causes me some distress… mostly embarrassment and feeling self-conscious. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there something about your appearance, aside from the scar, that you feel is flawed or defective?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I’m 64, so of course there are little things I don’t love, but nothing I’d call flawed or defective.\n\nIt’s really just the scar that’s been on my mind. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the scar makes you imperfect in some way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I feel silly saying it that way. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. So I suppose it does make me feel… less put together, maybe imperfect. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about the scar when it pops into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it can be difficult, though I wouldn’t say it takes over completely.\n\nIt’s more that once I notice it, or even think about being at church or bridge, my mind sort of goes back to it. So yes, it can be hard to shake off sometimes. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the scar been interfering with your ability to concentrate on other things you need to do?\n\nPatient: A bit, yes… I hate to admit that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m making excuses.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in a couple of times because I get distracted fussing over my wrist or thinking about whether someone might notice it. But when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nI’m not sure if that sounds silly, but… yes, I do think about it more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if the scar were gone, you would no longer feel self-conscious about how others perceive you?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe I would feel less self-conscious, yes. I don’t want to say it would fix everything about how I feel, because that sounds a little too neat.\n\nBut the scar is what I keep coming back to. I know it’s probably not as noticeable as it seems to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to my wrist, like that’s the first thing they see now. So if it weren’t there… I suppose I’d feel more at ease. Does that make sense?\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your worry about the scar affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’ve been late turning in bookkeeping work and have skipped some choir and theater volunteer commitments because I get so caught up trying to make sure my wrist won’t be noticed.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.

With family and friends, I suppose it’s more that I’ve pulled back from some things. I’ve skipped a few choir and theater volunteer commitments because I get so caught up trying to make sure my wrist won’t be noticed. And with work, I’ve been late turning in some bookkeeping things for the same reason.

I know that’s not exactly a relationship problem, but it does affect how dependable I am, I guess… which I don’t feel good about. Does that answer what you mean?

But I have been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work because I get sort of caught up trying to make sure my wrist is covered or not noticeable before I go in or send things off.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped a few choir practices and some theater volunteer things, which I don’t like admitting. It’s just… I start worrying people will notice it, and then it feels easier not to go. But at church or bridge, I still worry people are noticing it. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I suppose that’s more about the appearance than anything it “means,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist with the scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly just the wrist, yes. I don’t really have other parts of my body I worry about in the same way. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is likely not as bad as it looks to me, and I’m trying not to make too much of it.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe they don’t really see it that way, but it feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about your scar is causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. Yes… mainly the scar on my wrist. I know it’s probably not as bad or as noticeable as it seems to me, and I don’t want to make too much of it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I almost feel silly bringing it up, but... I’ve been having a hard time not thinking about this scar on my wrist. I know it’s probably not as noticeable as it seems to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I feel like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you help with, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your scar been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still managing, mostly. But I have been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work because I get sort of caught up trying to make sure my wrist is covered or not noticeable before I go in or send things off.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped a few choir practices and some theater volunteer things, which I don’t like admitting. It’s just… I start worrying people will notice it, and then it feels easier not to go. That probably sounds a bit silly, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the scar, even when you're not in situations where it might be noticed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I try not to make too much of it. It just sort of pops into my mind, even when I’m not doing anything where anyone would see it. I’ll think, “It’s probably not really that bad,” but then I picture being at church or playing bridge and feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me. I know that may not be entirely reasonable, but it’s hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they noticed the scar?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’d judge me exactly… maybe that sounds too strong. It’s more that I keep feeling like if they notice it, their eyes will go right to my wrist and that’ll be the first thing they see about me now. \n\nI do know, at least in my head, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. But at church or bridge, I still worry people are noticing it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of the scar that bothers you, or is it more about what you think it might mean to others about you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly the way it looks, if I’m being honest. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I suppose that’s more about the appearance than anything it “means,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist with the scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly just the wrist, yes. I don’t really have other parts of my body I worry about in the same way. I mean, I know the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it… like it’s the first thing they notice now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the appearance of the scar that bothers you, such as its size, shape, or color?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s one specific thing like the shape or color… maybe just that it’s there and visible on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not as noticeable as it feels to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just get this feeling that people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe that sounds a bit silly, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar's appearance is greater than how others actually perceive it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it probably is. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is likely not as bad as it looks to me, and I’m trying not to make too much of it.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe they don’t really see it that way, but it feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about your scar is causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. But it does bother me more than I’d like to admit, I suppose.\n\nI know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice now. So yes, it causes me some distress… mostly embarrassment and feeling self-conscious. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there something about your appearance, aside from the scar, that you feel is flawed or defective?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I’m 64, so of course there are little things I don’t love, but nothing I’d call flawed or defective.\n\nIt’s really just the scar that’s been on my mind. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the scar makes you imperfect in some way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I feel silly saying it that way. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. So I suppose it does make me feel… less put together, maybe imperfect. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about the scar when it pops into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it can be difficult, though I wouldn’t say it takes over completely.\n\nIt’s more that once I notice it, or even think about being at church or bridge, my mind sort of goes back to it. I’ll tell myself, “It’s probably not as noticeable as I think,” but then I still feel like everyone’s eyes are going right to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they see. So yes, it can be hard to shake off sometimes. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the scar been interfering with your ability to concentrate on other things you need to do?\n\nPatient: A bit, yes… I hate to admit that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m making excuses.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in a couple of times because I get distracted fussing over my wrist or thinking about whether someone might notice it. And I’ve skipped some choir and theater volunteer things too, just because I got so caught up trying to make sure it wouldn’t show.\n\nIt’s not constant, but… yes, it has gotten in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about the scar ever feel like it's intruding into your thoughts, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t want to overstate it, because I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me.\n\nBut it does sort of pop in when I’m trying to pay attention to something else, especially if I’m thinking about church or bridge. I’ll start wondering if people are looking at it, like their eyes go right to my wrist and that’s the first thing they notice about me now. I try to tell myself that’s probably not true, but the thought still comes in anyway. Is that what you mean by intruding?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the scar more often than you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than I’d like, I suppose.\n\nI know, realistically, the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. But when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nI’m not sure if that sounds silly, but… yes, I do think about it more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if the scar were gone, you would no longer feel self-conscious about how others perceive you?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe I would feel less self-conscious, yes. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I almost feel silly bringing it up, but... I’ve been having a hard time not thinking about this scar on my wrist. I know it’s probably not as noticeable as it seems to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I feel like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you help with, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your scar been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still managing, mostly. But I have been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work because I get sort of caught up trying to make sure my wrist is covered or not noticeable before I go in or send things off.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped a few choir practices and some theater volunteer things, which I don’t like admitting. It’s just… I start worrying people will notice it, and then it feels easier not to go. That probably sounds a bit silly, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the scar, even when you're not in situations where it might be noticed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I try not to make too much of it. It just sort of pops into my mind, even when I’m not doing anything where anyone would see it. I’ll think, “It’s probably not really that bad,” but then I picture being at church or playing bridge and feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me. I know that may not be entirely reasonable, but it’s hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they noticed the scar?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’d judge me exactly… maybe that sounds too strong. It’s more that I keep feeling like if they notice it, their eyes will go right to my wrist and that’ll be the first thing they see about me now. \n\nI do know, at least in my head, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. But at church or bridge, I still worry people are noticing it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of the scar that bothers you, or is it more about what you think it might mean to others about you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly the way it looks, if I’m being honest. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I suppose that’s more about the appearance than anything it “means,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist with the scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly just the wrist, yes. I don’t really have other parts of my body I worry about in the same way. I mean, I know the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it… like it’s the first thing they notice now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the appearance of the scar that bothers you, such as its size, shape, or color?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s one specific thing like the shape or color… maybe just that it’s there and visible on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not as noticeable as it feels to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just get this feeling that people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe that sounds a bit silly, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar's appearance is greater than how others actually perceive it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it probably is. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is likely not as bad as it looks to me, and I’m trying not to make too much of it.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe they don’t really see it that way, but it feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about your scar is causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. But it does bother me more than I’d like to admit, I suppose.\n\nI know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice now. So yes, it causes me some distress… mostly embarrassment and feeling self-conscious. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there something about your appearance, aside from the scar, that you feel is flawed or defective?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I’m 64, so of course there are little things I don’t love, but nothing I’d call flawed or defective.\n\nIt’s really just the scar that’s been on my mind. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the scar makes you imperfect in some way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I feel silly saying it that way. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. So I suppose it does make me feel… less put together, maybe imperfect. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about the scar when it pops into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it can be difficult, though I wouldn’t say it takes over completely.\n\nIt’s more that once I notice it, or even think about being at church or bridge, my mind sort of goes back to it. I’ll tell myself, “It’s probably not as noticeable as I think,” but then I still feel like everyone’s eyes are going right to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they see. So yes, it can be hard to shake off sometimes. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the scar been interfering with your ability to concentrate on other things you need to do?\n\nPatient: A bit, yes… I hate to admit that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m making excuses.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in a couple of times because I get distracted fussing over my wrist or thinking about whether someone might notice it. And I’ve skipped some choir and theater volunteer things too, just because I got so caught up trying to make sure it wouldn’t show.\n\nIt’s not constant, but… yes, it has gotten in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about the scar ever feel like it's intruding into your thoughts, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t want to overstate it, because I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me.\n\nBut it does sort of pop in when I’m trying to pay attention to something else, especially if I’m thinking about church or bridge. I’ll start wondering if people are looking at it, like their eyes go right to my wrist and that’s the first thing they notice about me now. I try to tell myself that’s probably not true, but the thought still comes in anyway. Is that what you mean by intruding?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the scar more often than you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than I’d like, I suppose.\n\nI know, realistically, the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. But when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nI’m not sure if that sounds silly, but… yes, I do think about it more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if the scar were gone, you would no longer feel self-conscious about how others perceive you?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe I would feel less self-conscious, yes. I don’t want to say it would fix everything about how I feel, because that sounds a little too neat.\n\nBut the scar is what I keep coming back to. I know it’s probably not as noticeable as it seems to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to my wrist, like that’s the first thing they see now. So if it weren’t there… I suppose I’d feel more at ease. I’ve skipped a few choir and theater volunteer commitments because I get so caught up trying to make sure my wrist won’t be noticed. I’ll think, “It’s probably not really that bad,” but then I picture being at church or playing bridge and feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me. I know that may not be entirely reasonable, but it’s hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they noticed the scar?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’d judge me exactly… maybe that sounds too strong. It’s more that I keep feeling like if they notice it, their eyes will go right to my wrist and that’ll be the first thing they see about me now. \n\nI do know, at least in my head, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. But at church or bridge, I still worry people are noticing it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of the scar that bothers you, or is it more about what you think it might mean to others about you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly the way it looks, if I’m being honest. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I suppose that’s more about the appearance than anything it “means,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist with the scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly just the wrist, yes. I don’t really have other parts of my body I worry about in the same way. I mean, I know the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it… like it’s the first thing they notice now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the appearance of the scar that bothers you, such as its size, shape, or color?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s one specific thing like the shape or color… maybe just that it’s there and visible on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not as noticeable as it feels to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just get this feeling that people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe that sounds a bit silly, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar's appearance is greater than how others actually perceive it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it probably is. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is likely not as bad as it looks to me, and I’m trying not to make too much of it.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Maybe they don’t really see it that way, but it feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about your scar is causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. But it does bother me more than I’d like to admit, I suppose.\n\nI know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice now. So yes, it causes me some distress… mostly embarrassment and feeling self-conscious. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there something about your appearance, aside from the scar, that you feel is flawed or defective?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I’m 64, so of course there are little things I don’t love, but nothing I’d call flawed or defective.\n\nIt’s really just the scar that’s been on my mind. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the scar makes you imperfect in some way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I feel silly saying it that way. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me.\n\nBut when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. So I suppose it does make me feel… less put together, maybe imperfect. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about the scar when it pops into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it can be difficult, though I wouldn’t say it takes over completely.\n\nIt’s more that once I notice it, or even think about being at church or bridge, my mind sort of goes back to it. I’ll tell myself, “It’s probably not as noticeable as I think,” but then I still feel like everyone’s eyes are going right to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they see. So yes, it can be hard to shake off sometimes. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the scar been interfering with your ability to concentrate on other things you need to do?\n\nPatient: A bit, yes… I hate to admit that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m making excuses.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in a couple of times because I get distracted fussing over my wrist or thinking about whether someone might notice it. And I’ve skipped some choir and theater volunteer things too, just because I got so caught up trying to make sure it wouldn’t show.\n\nIt’s not constant, but… yes, it has gotten in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about the scar ever feel like it's intruding into your thoughts, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t want to overstate it, because I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me.\n\nBut it does sort of pop in when I’m trying to pay attention to something else, especially if I’m thinking about church or bridge. I’ll start wondering if people are looking at it, like their eyes go right to my wrist and that’s the first thing they notice about me now. I try to tell myself that’s probably not true, but the thought still comes in anyway. Is that what you mean by intruding?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the scar more often than you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than I’d like, I suppose.\n\nI know, realistically, the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. But when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nI’m not sure if that sounds silly, but… yes, I do think about it more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if the scar were gone, you would no longer feel self-conscious about how others perceive you?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe I would feel less self-conscious, yes. I don’t want to say it would fix everything about how I feel, because that sounds a little too neat.\n\nBut the scar is what I keep coming back to. I know it’s probably not as noticeable as it seems to me, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to my wrist, like that’s the first thing they see now. So if it weren’t there… I suppose I’d feel more at ease. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Has your worry about the scar affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nWith family and friends, I suppose it’s more that I’ve pulled back from some things. I’ve skipped a few choir and theater volunteer commitments because I get so caught up trying to make sure my wrist won’t be noticed. And with work, I’ve been late turning in some bookkeeping things for the same reason.\n\nI know that’s not exactly a relationship problem, but it does affect how dependable I am, I guess… which I don’t feel good about. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your appearance, related to the scar or otherwise, that you feel is flawed or defective and that you think others might notice?\n\nPatient: Yes… mainly the scar on my wrist. I suppose that’s the part that feels… flawed, if that’s the word. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the scar makes you feel imperfect in a way that you believe others would notice or care about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say.
